[T&A 29Oct09] OK [JLM] 2 The prophets didn't really write the “Good News”, but they wrote about it in part in the Holy Scriptures, and their writings about it was like God's promise to us. In other words, the Good News was not “written” long ago, it was only “promised” long ago. Your translation here implies that the gospel was in the OT, but it is in the NT. The OT only promised that it was coming. I think this could be more accurate if you would follow NLT and GNT more closely. I would suggest adding a period at the end of verse 1 and then translating, “This Good News was promised long ago by God in the Holy Scriptures which he caused his prophets to write” (or more simply, “by means of his prophets” if that is as clear). Then you would also need to begin a new sentence at v3, like the NLT and GNT did. It is the same thing but still we made adjustments following your idea. We cannot end v1 because we need to have “meupiyan panugtulen” in v3 to avoid confusion where “meyitenged” goes with. [T&A 17Feb09] I don't think this is much better. The problem is “impasulat” because it implies that the prophets wrote the Good News. They did NOT. They promised that there would be Good News in the future. Matthew, Mark, John, and Luke wrote the Good News, and they are not the “prophets” being referred to. Suggest: ne insaad dengan te Manama pinaahi te me prupita diye te Matulus ne Kasulatan. (or maybe move the “pinaahi te me prupita” to the end of the sentence) [JLM] OK. [T&A 4May09] 3 Although you have “neetew” that seems to underemphasize “according to the flesh” or “humanity”. [T&A 17Feb09] The ES say regarding “grace” that: “This is a hendiadys, one semantic concept expressed by two nouns connected by ‘and’, in this case meaning the grace of apostleship, the divine gift of being an apostle [AB, BECNT, ICC2, NICNT, NTC, TH, TNTC, St, SSA, WBC; CEV, NCV, NLT, REB, TEV] Since salvation is not in focus here, grace is not to be taken in the sense of God’s favor in granting salvation, but of God graciously appointing Paul to ministry [SSA] He has received the special gift or privilege of being an apostle.” So perhaps a better translation would be, “neyimu key ne apustulis tenged te keupiya te Manama eyew egpanulu…” If this sentence becomes too complicated, it may need to be divided into two. [T&A 6Oct08] To “bulig” (help) is too general to be used here for “encourage” unless you follow the TEV. The difference is that you say they will help one another so that their faith will be strengthened. Whatever that help is is ambiguous. It might be preaching, money, hard work, etc. The TEV says that they will “be helped” by each other's faith. So it is each other's faith that helps. See for example the KNEBT which makes this clear. The original says faith -> encouragement/help. You say help -> strong faith. The two are not the same. So if you want to use “bulig” instead of “bagget” or “dasig” here, you need to make an adjustment. [JLM] OK. Bagget is better and natural and more specific. [T&A 5May09] You have taken the minority opinion on this verse by assuming that the Gentiles will literally mock and insult the Jews. From the Exegetical Summaries: “QUESTION—Should the statement of this verse regarding Gentiles judging Jews be taken as a hypothetical situation or as a real one? 1. It should not be taken literally [BECNT, ICC2, Mu, NICNT, SSA, TH] This should not be understood to mean that Gentiles will literally have the role of judges, but rather that their obedience will be evidence against the Jew of what they could and ought to have been [BECNT, ICC2, Mu, SSA, TH] This does not mean that the Gentiles will themselves condemn the Jews, but that the fact that they have obeyed the commands of the Law will serve as a basis for the Jews being condemned [TH] This is probably not to be taken literally for it is God who will actually do the condemning; their example will show that God is right in condemning [SSA] Paul’s intent here is to emphasize the equality of Jew and Gentile with respect to judgment [NICNT] 2. It should probably be taken literally, meaning that believing Gentiles will stand up in the judgment and condemn unbelieving Jews [AB, Gdt, Mor] This context speaks of Gentile Christians condemning Jews by their fulfillment of the law; it would be a bitter thing for the Jews to hear that they will not only be judged like the Gentiles, but also by them [Gdt]” So as you can see, you have taken position #2 which is not well supported. Are things normally “impamalehetan” before or after they happen? In other words, I can egpamalehetan that what someone says is true after they say it, but can I egpamalehetan it before they say it? In this case, these prophets did this long before God revealed this new way of righteousness through faith, so have you chosen the right word? The Greek word is often translated “witness”, but the meaning is “to provide information about a person or an event concerning which the speaker has direct knowledge.” (L&N). The BTs translated “bear witness” as “spoken of”, “was written”, “was prophesied.” The NLT translated as “was promised.” I suggest you consider one of these instead of “impamalehetan” in order to concord better. [T&A 6May09] I also wonder about the last clause, “egpekeyimu te ware pad neyimu.” This clause most likely refers to the fact that God used the perfect tense, which means already completed, when he said “I have made you the father of many nations.” In other words, God called Abraham “the father of many nations” before Isaac was even born! So God, knowing the future and his immutable plan for Abraham, called Abraham a father of many nations before he even had children! God called the things that were not as though they were. So the meaning here has less to do with God creating, and more to do with God speaking of things not yet true as though they were already true. Although your interpretation is also possible, it seems less likely in context, but you have especially not translated “calls”. “Refers to speaking of things not yet existent as if they already existed. A, Ba, EGT, Hd, L, NIC, V, W; KJV, 20, Ty, Wey. (NIC) regards “the things which are not” as referring to the things determined by God to come to pass but which have not yet been fulfilled. These things do not yet exist, but since determined by God they are “called” by him as having existence. (V) God speaks of, and so treats, non-existent things, upon which He has determined, as being already existent. (Ba) The argument of the apostle is founded in part on the fact that the past tense is used – I have made thee – and that God spoke of a thing as already done which he had promised or purposed to do. The sense is, he had, in his mind or purpose, constituted him the father of many nations; and so certain was the fulfilment of the divine purposes, that he spoke of it as already accomplished.” (EH) Suggest: “He talks about what is not yet true as though it is already true.” (Similar to NIV) or “He says that what has not yet happened has already happened.” (egpakakahi ne ka ware pad neyitabu, neyitabu e) [JLM] Ok. We are convinced with profound explanation. We changed it to “egpakakahi ne duen e ka ware pad” which is more general than having neyitabu which limits “happenings” only. [T&A 16Apr09] This is very figurative language. The meaning is simply David's prayer that the good things that his enemies delighted in (figuratively: feasts) would become a curse (figuratively: trap, snare, pitfall) to them instead of a blessing and thus they would be repaid (retribution) for their evil deeds. Paul is applying this quotation from the Psalms to the Jews and their love of and devotion to the law which has become a snare to them. Since this verse is highly figurative, you are free to translate the meaning, to combine things (e.g. trap, snare, pitfall can be combined into one or two), or to translate fairly literally if it conveys the same meaning in a natural way. You have translated fairly literally in both cases, but only you can answer the question of naturalness. I suggest you at least translate “feast” less literally. (Yakan: pleasures; SJM: what they are devoted to) “(EGT) Their table in the Psalm is that in which they delight.” [JLM] We prefer literal translation because this is a special passage but we see to it that the meaning is clear. [T&A 20Apr09] I picked the ALT because I thought it was more natural and clearer. Is that OK with you? If so, delete the original. [JLM] OK we deleted the original.
